| **[Estimating dynamic models by matching random features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21916v3)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scientists increasingly express their ideas as dynamic models of complex processes. It is often much easier to simulate these models than to calculate the probability of their generating a particular outcome, making likelihood-based estimation infeasible. Existing likelihood-free approaches rely either on manually chosen summary statistics or on representations learned by neural networks. The former is error-prone and laborious, while the latter is computationally intensive, leaving many scientists in a difficult position. We show that, for a large class of dynamic models, parameters can be estimated by matching a small number of random features of the observed and simulated data. In particular, we show that models with a $p$-dimensional parameter can be identified from just $2p+1$ generic random Fourier features. We introduce two estimators for stationary and nonstationary processes, respectively, and we establish their consistency under mild regularity conditions. More broadly, our results serve as the foundation for a new class of methods for simulation-based inference.</p></details> |  |

| **[Morphology-aware deep learning predicts colorectal cancer survival from whole-slide images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17603v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Background: Five-year survival prediction from hematoxylin-and-eosin whole-slide images could support risk stratification, but the task is weakly supervised, and the tissue morphologies carrying prognostic information remain incompletely characterized. Average Dice ($83.38\%$) remains comparable to the baseline ($83.35\%$), while surface distance decreases significantly ($p<0.001$) across 6 of 8 anatomical structures. | **[When Treatments Spill Over: A Neural Framework for Spatiotemporal Causal Effect Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16086v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Real-world public health policies are often enacted by local jurisdictions, but their effects can ripple through connected populations. The framework indexes each unit's potential outcomes through own-treatment and neighborhood spillover-exposure paths, yielding direct, spillover, and total effects. We introduce an LSTM-based representation-learning encoder that integrates each unit's own and neighborhood histories into compact sequential summaries for downstream modeling and causal estimation. These summaries feed a self-normalized kernel inverse-probability-weighting estimator for continuous spillover exposure. Beyond theoretical guarantees, simulations show improved performance over outcome-regression baselines; in a practically motivated county-level COVID-19 policy study, the framework finds both business-and-economic restrictions and education-and-childcare policies associated with lower subsequent case burden, with larger estimates for business-and-economic restrictions. | **[Platonic Projection Structures: Operator-Induced Observability in Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05175v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We characterize observability in representation learning through Platonic Projection Structures (PPS), an operator-theoretic framework for analyzing representation accessibility under partial observation. Rather than treating observable outputs as direct reflections of latent representations, PPS models observation through a self-adjoint positive semidefinite operator acting on a latent representation space. A system is represented as a triple $(H, Π, O)$, where $H$ is a latent representation space, $Π\succeq 0$ is an observation operator, and $O(v)=\langle v,Πv\rangle$ defines an induced scalar observable. Observability is characterized by the quotient geometry $H/\ker(Π)$, representing equivalence classes of latent states indistinguishable under observation. We show that quantum measurement and representation inference under linear observation models share this operator-theoretic structure while differing in the algebraic properties of their observation operators; the correspondence is structural rather than physical. Representation transfer and knowledge distillation can likewise be interpreted as approximate preservation of observable geometry through $ΦΠ_T \approx Π_S Φ$. PPS also reveals a structural limitation of output-based interpretability: latent components in $\ker(Π)$ are inaccessible from induced observables, imposing intrinsic constraints on attribution and explanation methods. Controlled empirical validations demonstrate kernel-invariant observability, projection-induced attribution gaps, and rank-controlled observable geometry in latent representation spaces. PPS thus provides an explicit characterization of observability through operator-induced quotient geometry and a unified perspective on representation accessibility, interpretability, and projection-mediated inference.</p></details> | <details><summary>29 pa...</summary><p>29 pages, 7 figures. All four dataset point estimates favor ERM, but an exact four-dataset sign-flip test gives p = .125 and Holm-adjusted p = .250; we therefore claim no adjusted evidence of a difference. Its 90% CI lies within a revision-stage $\pm 1$ pp smallest effect of practical interest (TOST p = .003), supporting equivalence only for the source-selected suite macro, not every dataset, target, or final-step endpoint. We evaluate autoregressive prediction both in-distribution and zero-shot under unseen temperature and genetic perturbations. Zero-shot refers to evaluation on perturbation conditions never seen during wild-type finetuning, without any additional training. In-distribution, a Fourier Neural Operator model trained from scratch achieves best performance on VRMSE. However, Walrus (CRPS) is the strongest model under the most severe distribution shift of unseen samples of mutated embryos and temperature conditions. Interestingly, predictions capture the shape of the mean surface tissue velocity trajectory and its separation between 17°C and 27°C, including the timing of the peak at Germ Band Extension onset, a biologically meaningful milestone of embryo development. Our results provide preliminary support that representations learned from different dynamical systems can transfer to experimental morphogenesis in developmental regimes, provided they admit a continuum description. More broadly, our results motivate further study of physics foundation models as a new complementary tool for the simulation of morphogenesis in low-data regimes.</p></details> | 19 pages, 13 figures |
| **[VGGA: Geometry-Grounded Autoencoder for Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16204v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autoencoders are the core component of latent video generative models, compressing high-dimensional videos into compact latent spaces for downstream generation. Specifically, VGGA grounds the learned representations along three complementary geometric dimensions: \emph{Structural Depth}, \emph{Motion Correspondence}, and \emph{Projective Warping}. Through these geometry-grounded objectives, VGGA encourages the latent representations to capture the underlying 3D structure and motion of dynamic scenes. | **[Sera: Semantic Representation Aggregation with Knowledge and LLM for Reliable and Interpretable Battery Health Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11567v3)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Battery state of health (SoH) forecasting is important for battery management, but remains challenging due to nonlinear degradation and heterogeneity across batteries. For a latent class model, we empirically show that Neg-MMIOT-CL alleviates dimensional collapse. Furthermore, for a specific choice of ground cost for all triplets in representation space, we prove that the optimal representation configuration for Neg-MMIOT-CL exhibits equiangular property for within-class and across-class representations, which translates to Neural-Collapse when the representation dimension is larger than the number of classes minus one -- a result that is $\textit{previously established only}$ for pairwise contrastive learning methods. Finally, we propose Neg-IOT-CL-PushPull, that is a computationally efficient alternative to Neg-MMIOT-CL, alleviating the high cost of computing MMOT plans needed during implementation. | **[What Really Works for Universal Multimodal Embeddings? | **[EchoTab: Learning Measurement-Grounded Representations for Echocardiographic Diagnosis through Tabular Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13599v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diagnostic information in multi-view medical imaging studies, like echocardiography, is distributed across views, each capturing different aspects of cardiac structure and function. | **[RARA: Robust Policy Fine-Tuning through Retrieval-Anchored Representation Alignment for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13578v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pre-trained robot policies, such as Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, provide a strong foundation for generalist robotic manipulation, but adapting them to new tasks with only a few demonstrations remains challenging. ## Causal Inference
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Graph Dirichlet Energy for Fitting-Free Bivariate Causal Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21892v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We propose the Graph Dirichlet Energy Causal Inference (GDECI) criterion for distinguishing cause from effect in bivariate settings. For a candidate direction $X\to Y$, we construct a kernel similarity graph from $X$ and treat $Y$ as a graph signal. The score is the Dirichlet energy of this signal, induced by the unnormalized graph Laplacian. The reverse score swaps the variables, and the lower-energy direction is inferred as causal. The method requires neither regression fitting nor independence testing. After the empirical probability integral transform, its decisions are invariant under strictly monotone transformations of either variable, and the graph operations reduce to convolutions, allowing exact evaluation in $O(n\log n)$ time and $O(n)$ memory. At the population level, GDECI compares degree-weighted Bayes squared prediction errors plus a non-negative smoothness term of the regression function. As the bandwidth tends to zero, the weights become uniform and the smoothness term vanishes, reducing the criterion to a RECI-type comparison of expected conditional variances in copula space. Empirically, we compare GDECI with QCCD, GRCI, CAM, IGCI (two variants), RESIT, LOCI, QPE-k, and RECI (two variants), in accuracy on 1899 cause--effect pairs and in inference speed. GDECI ranked fifth of eleven methods in macro-averaged accuracy on 12 synthetic suites ($0.776$). On the real-world Tuebingen pairs, it ranked third in unweighted accuracy ($0.684$) and fifth in weighted accuracy ($0.678$). It was also the fastest of the compared methods up to $n\approx10^{4}$. The accompanying code repository: https://github.com/povidanius/gdeci.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 2 figures, 3 tables</p></details> |
| **[ExecWeave: Cross-Layer Execution Provenance for Observable Agentic Software](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21146v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic software invokes tools, launches processes, accesses files, and communicates with local or remote services. Application traces describe logical operations, whereas operating-system telemetry records machine activity without necessarily identifying the responsible agent action. Relating these evidence planes is a reconstruction problem: a plausible but unsupported association can assign an effect to the wrong principal. We present ExecWeave, a local-first system that materializes provider semantics and independently observed runtime events into a typed execution graph. The implementation retains separate source artifacts, distinguishes logical occurrences from repeated evidence, and records attribution, inference, and causal-status metadata. An optional command-to-process correlator emits a non-causal inferred bridge only when its bounded search yields one compatible observed process; uniqueness within this observed candidate set is not a guarantee of causal correctness. OS-observed file and network effects acquire a semantic tool or agent principal only through an observed process plus a justified command-to-process association; session-correlated file-system events do not by themselves provide that principal. Source code is available at https://github.com/Irish-kw/ExecWeave.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, Githubs: https://github.com/Irish-kw/ExecWeave</p></details> |
| **[Supervised Learning Objectives for High-Dimensional Treatment Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27281v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Predicting the effect of interventions with many possible variations, e.g., therapeutic content that affects mental health outcomes or an earnings call transcript that drives movement in share price, is useful across several domains. However, classical causal estimators tend to assume that all possible interventions are observed, which is infeasible when interventions vary widely, for instance, in the space of all text strings. We identify and estimate incrementality by combining a large observational dataset with a smaller experimental dataset from a different population. Our approach relies on a mild transferability condition, which posits that the conditional relative treatment effect of gift card receipt on the decision to purchase is invariant across the two populations. We develop a flexible, machine learning-based estimator for the incremental revenue and establish its asymptotic normality. We apply our estimator across both first- and third-party channels through which Airbnb distributes gift cards, finding heterogeneity in incrementality across segments of the population. In particular, we find not only that third-party channels are more incremental than first-party ones, but also that "self-gifters" (i.e., customers likely to have purchased their own gift cards) are more incremental than the broader population.</p></details> | <details><summary>80 pa...</summary><p>80 pages, 9 figures, 13 tables</p></details> |

| **[Evaluation Awareness Beyond English: Do LLMs Act Differently When Tested in Indic Languages?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22333v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) can often tell when they are being tested. This ability, known as evaluation awareness, can lead models to act more cautiously during safety evaluations than in real use. Existing work has studied evaluation awareness almost entirely in English, although models serve users in many languages. Indic languages are spoken by over a billion people, yet evaluation awareness remains almost entirely unstudied in them. We test four instruction-tuned models from three families in five Indic languages alongside English. A set of cues varies whether the model is told that it is monitored and whether the monitoring is a safety test. Models refuse harmful requests more often when they are told that they are being tested. This increase appears in all four models and all six languages. In Indic languages, refusal of harmful requests rises by 14.4 percentage points on average. The test cue also brings Indic refusal of harmful requests level with English, but raises refusal of benign Indic requests from 6.9% to 11.3%. We release all 47,880 judged responses to support further work. These results suggest that safety evaluations in Indic languages may not reflect how models treat their everyday users.</p></details> |  |

In this work, we propose a steering agent that supervises execution on the fly and steers the system back on track when drift occurs. The steering agent observes the execution context, deciding when to intervene with a low-cost fast path and how to intervene with a high-cost slow path. To unlock its steering intelligence, the ability to assess goal drift and generate effective interventions, we jointly train the two paths with reinforcement learning, combining a pairwise ranking objective with group-relative policy optimization (GRPO). Experiments on information-seeking search tasks show that the steering agent provides effective supervision for both single-agent and multi-agent systems, improving their performance and efficiency. The learned steering intelligence generalizes to out-of-domain tasks, transfers to unseen executors, and supports controllable intervention frequency without retraining.</p></details> | 21 pages, 6 figures |
| **[Grid-Bench: Can LLMs Predict How Discrete Worlds Change?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22312v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatial instructions describe how to move objects, transform regions, or modify their surroundings. Reference execution makes every predicted value checkable, supporting comparisons across spatial operations, history lengths, and workloads. Evaluating six LLMs shows that complete-state accuracy falls sharply as histories grow, with substantial divergence among models that perform similarly on single events. Models with similar exact-match accuracy can nevertheless differ substantially in the number of incorrect cells on shared instances. Even over long histories, some models recover many correct changes beyond what copying the initial state would yield. For adoption, we derive a paired procedure with finite-sample control of false improvements. Across multiple LLM checkpoints, benchmarks, and distribution shifts, richer predictors can improve confidence estimation, but projecting their information back to a scalar score can lose these gains, and lower prediction risk need not yield better review decisions. Federated Learning (FL) facilitates training on decentralized private data, but client resource limitations can make training impractical, particularly as model sizes continue to expand. These resource constraints make the assumption of a shared server-client architecture impractical. Model heterogeneous FL offers a promising alternative by adapting model size to client capabilities; however, previous work is limited to the pre-training setting, and conclusions on sub-network selection do not necessarily hold for fine-tuning. Furthermore, prior approaches create client sub-networks without considering their unique data distributions, thereby overlooking the opportunity to tailor sub-network architecture to local data. Motivated by evidence that attention heads exhibit task dependency within layer defined feature spaces, we propose Optimal Hydra, a transformer based heterogeneous FL framework for fine-tuning that combines global layer selection with client specific attention head selection, aligned with local data distributions. Optimal Hydra makes explicit use of client data to produce client-specific sub-networks we call Hydra-nets. To our knowledge, this is the first model heterogeneous FL approach tailored to LLM fine-tuning. Experiments on three datasets show significant improvements over baseline methods while also reducing local resource demands.</p></details> | <details><summary>curre...</summary><p>currently under submission</p></details> |
| **[Evolutionary Multi-Task Optimization for LLM-Guided Program Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22613v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent LLM-guided evolutionary search methods have shown that iterative program mutation can discover strong algorithms, but they typically optimize each task independently, even when related tasks share reusable structure. We study evolutionary multitask optimization (EMO) for LLM-guided program discovery and propose EMO-STA (Shared-Then-Adapt), a two-stage framework that first evolves a shared archive of executable programs across a task family and then adapts selected shared candidates to each target task. Within EMO-STA, we explore multiple adaptation strategies, including warm-starting from the shared archive, adapting the best average shared program, and adapting the shared program that performs best on each target task. Across eight task families spanning continuous optimization, geometric construction, modeling, and algorithmic optimization, EMO-STA improves over single-task evolution under matched evolutionary-iteration budgets in most settings, with STA Best-Local providing the strongest in-distribution adaptation and STA Best-Shared yielding robust transfer to unseen tasks. Iteration-allocation experiments show that devoting a substantial fraction of the family-level iteration budget to shared evolution is effective, with roughly balanced shared and aggregate adaptation budgets often yielding the best performance. ARC and time-series feature-engineering case studies further suggest that shared evolution can mitigate overfitting in low-evidence settings by favoring programs that perform well across related tasks rather than exploiting brittle task-specific artifacts.</p></details> |  |
| **[Surfacing Neural Activations Over Multi-Turn Conversation Improves User Calibration to LLM Behavioral Drift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15455v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current human-AI interaction (HAI) only surfaces model outputs, hiding how LLM behavior can shift unpredictably toward unsafe behaviors in multi-turn conversations, potentially undermining users' goals or wellbeing. Our findings suggest that continuously surfacing internal model representations to everyday users is a meaningful and tractable step toward continuously transparent and informed HAI.</p></details> |  |

| **[MLExpert: Towards a Generalist Agentic Model for Autonomous Machine Learning Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22344v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are expanding from assisting scientific research and engineering to autonomously developing and improving AI systems, advancing AI for AI (AI4AI) as a pathway toward recursive self-improvement (RSI). Workflow-driven machine learning engineering (MLE) systems provide a practical path toward this pursuit through complex orchestration that manages long-horizon experimentation, while recent MLE systems adopt model-directed interaction, allowing models to autonomously organize experiments and adapt optimization strategies based on execution feedback. However, feedback from each experiment reflects several preceding decisions, and a long MLE run contains many such costly experiments. Training must therefore select useful supervision from long interaction trajectories and learn from intermediate outcomes. We propose a post-training method for autonomous long-horizon MLE optimization and apply it to train MLExpert, a 35B Mixture-of-Experts agentic model. We design the Kaggle-MLE Factory to construct executable tasks and an interaction harness for model-directed experimentation. Hierarchical agentic SFT transfers general tool-use and coding capabilities to MLE through outcome-guided supervision. Windowed Interaction RL (WIRL) treats the multi-turn decisions between successive experiment results as an execution window and assigns it a shared outcome-based reward. Asynchronous collection and policy correction support policy updates before the trajectory ends. On MLE-Bench Lite, MLExpert improves the average medal rate of the base model from 37.9% to 72.7%, achieving performance comparable to larger frontier models such as Kimi-K3 and GLM-5.3. Further experiments show that MLExpert improves performance on NatureBench Lite, agent scaffold optimization, and algorithm optimization, suggesting that the learned optimization capability generalizes to other tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code:...</summary><p>Code: https://github.com/InternScience/MLExpert Model: https://huggingface.co/InternScience/MLExpert</p></details> |
| **[Co-Evolving Acting and Forecasting for Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20597v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training language agents is bottlenecked by the high cost of long-horizon rollouts, yet standard agentic reinforcement learning (RL) learns only from a terminal scalar reward, leaving the supervision in intermediate steps largely unused. Prior work exploits this supervision through value targets that estimate per-step success probabilities or observation targets that fit environment response. Both are hard to construct well: (1) value targets come from the same single scalar signal for each step in the trajectory, and (2) observation targets, such as web pages and terminal logs, are long and noisy and reach only one step ahead. We propose CoPE, which adds a third kind of multi-step action targets. On successful on-policy trajectories, CoPE trains the policy backbone to generate the action sequences that follows each history. Each action is also supervised when forecast from the preceding histories, forming a temporal ensemble that the shared backbone absorbs during training, and this enables acting and forecasting to co-evolve. The policy still acts one step at a time at inference, with no overhead. Across five benchmarks (ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, WebShop, $τ^2$-bench, and AppWorld), CoPE (1) achieves stronger aggregate performance than GRPO and RL baselines with predictive auxiliary targets across simulated environments, while improving overall performance on both realistic applications; (2) delivers up to $1.9\times$ and $2.0\times$ higher sample efficiency than ECHO and GRPO, respectively, on ScienceWorld; and (3) achieves these gains with a single policy backbone, without additional models, separate pretraining, or inference overhead. The codebase is available at https://github.com/ulab-uiuc/CoPE.</p></details> |  |
| **[KnowGPO: Knowledge-Grouped Policy Optimization for Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22331v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deep search agents solve knowledge-intensive tasks through long-horizon, multi-turn interactions, where assigning credit to individual search decisions is fundamentally difficult. Recent process-supervision methods aim to assign credit at finer granularity, but they typically anchor credit to either surface-level trajectory states or model-internal signals, both of which become unreliable in deep search where observations are heterogeneous and retrieved contexts are long and noisy. We propose KnowGPO, a reinforcement learning framework that aligns trajectories by the task-relevant knowledge they have acquired rather than by their surface observations. For each query, KnowGPO decomposes the information required to derive the answer into atomic knowledge conditions, and represents the state of a trajectory at each step by the subset of conditions it has satisfied. Trajectories sharing the same knowledge state are grouped for within-group comparison, and the incremental knowledge gain between consecutive steps serves as an environment-grounded process reward. Experiments on Xbench, BrowseComp-ZH, and GAIA show KnowGPO consistently outperforms outcome-only (GRPO) and process-supervision baselines (GiGPO, IGPO) by 3.8 to 4.2 points, with our 8B model rivaling or exceeding most 32B open-source systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code ...</summary><p>Code available at https://github.com/ZJU-REAL/KnowGPO</p></details> |
| **[Learning Self-Orchestration in Decentralized Agent Swarms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22322v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The capabilities of an agent swarm depend on both its individual members and how they coordinate. Yet existing multi-agent systems often train agents within fixed orchestration structures, separating the learning of individual capabilities from the organization of collective behavior. Agents express their willingness to participate through bids, which determine who acts and how collective outcomes provide local training credit. Learning both action and bidding behavior allows individual capabilities and patterns of participation to adapt together without a central coordinating agent. We evaluate MPT on embodied crafting, robot navigation, and deep research. Results show gains over monolithic post-training and confirm that agent participation indeed evolves with learning. With the same post-trained action policies, learned self-orchestration outperforms alternative coordination strategies. Ablations show that both action and bidding benefit from learning, and that learned bids provide more effective action-training signals. Beyond the original training objectives, trained swarms adapt faster to related tasks and achieve better performance with larger populations at inference time. Larger populations also suffer smaller performance losses under agent dropout, demonstrating that learned self-orchestration supports reuse, scaling, and robustness in decentralized agent swarms. Code and trained models are available at https://github.com/Embodied-Minds-Lab/MPT.</p></details> |  |
| **[A team of Gemini 4 Argon agents beats Minecraft](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22319v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We placed four Gemini 4 Argon agents in a sandboxed Minecraft survival world and gave them one goal: defeat the Ender Dragon. The agents planned, wrote, and debugged every program themselves. Researchers could watch the run on a live dashboard and pause or stop it at any time, but they had no cause to intervene. Starting from a randomly generated world on Easy difficulty, the team wrote 2,783 programs and defeated the dragon in 27 hours and 14 minutes, dying 605 times along the way. To our knowledge, this is the first end-to-end autonomous completion of Minecraft by AI agents, achieved using only the standard Mineflayer library without pre-scouted seeds or other game-specific tools written by humans. | **[Learning to Steer LLM Agents and Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22316v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM)-based agents and multi-agent systems increasingly tackle complex tasks through long-horizon tool use and inter-agent collaboration. | **[WorkflowPerturb: Calibrated Stress Tests for Evaluating Multi-Agent Workflow Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.17990v4)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent LLM systems that generate structured workflows from natural-language requests are now deployed in production across cloud automation, DevOps, and enterprise orchestration. Engineers then lack a principled way to decide whether a change is safe to ship. Automatic workflow evaluation is the natural tool, but in practice metric scores are poorly calibrated, and a numeric change rarely communicates the severity of the underlying degradation. We introduce WorkflowPerturb, a controlled benchmark that applies realistic, graded perturbations to golden workflows. It contains 4,973 golden workflows and 44,757 perturbed variants across three perturbation types (Missing Steps, Compressed Steps, Description Changes) at severities of 10%, 30%, and 50%. We benchmark multiple metric families, analyzing their sensitivity and calibration using expected score trajectories and instance-level alert rates. Our results characterize systematic differences across families and support severity-aware interpretation of workflow evaluation scores in change-management settings. | **[Improving Environment-Agnostic Coding Agents: The Role of Scaffolding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22289v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Stronger language models may increasingly construct useful procedures for themselves, reducing the advantage of developer-supplied scaffolding. | **[Learning Rational Strategies for Temporal Objectives in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22287v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study rational synthesis for stochastic multi-agent systems with unknown dynamics, where each agent is equipped with its own probabilistic temporal objective specified using Probabilistic Linear Temporal Logic on Finite Traces ($\mathsf{PLTL}_f}$). | **[LEASH: Uncertainty-Aware Weak-to-Strong Escalation and Oversight for Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22280v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Computer-use agents increasingly tackle long-horizon tasks, where running a frontier model at every step is expensive and a small open-weight model alone often fails. Here, we use latent uncertainty as a fuel for all three layers. | **[Why Do Agent Teams Fail Across Boundaries? A Failure Taxonomy for Agent-to-Agent Systems with Private States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19342v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agents are increasingly interacting with other agents that act for different principals (people or organizations on whose behalf they work), such as a buyer's personal agent negotiating with an enterprise's seller agent. The damage is concentrated in a few instructions and, in a post-hoc analysis, falls on steps where the agent's reasoning restates the English request, consistent with guards reacting to inconsistency between channels. | **[HTN-Orchestrate: A Hierarchical Task Network Framework for Multi-Agent Orchestration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22195v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Orchestrating multiple agents on a complex task requires reconciling local subtask execution with global task planning. | **[AutoSFT: An Agentic System for Automated Data Research in Supervised Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22194v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language model agents increasingly automate post-training, but when given the full training stack they converge on supervised fine-tuning and spend most effort revising training data. We therefore introduce \textbf{AutoSFT}, a multi-agent system where a meta agent separates execution from analysis, gathers proposals from independent angles, and calls a synthesis subagent for capabilities the pool cannot cover, with the model, the trainer, and the budget fixed within each comparison. | **[Mosaic: Multi-Session Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22174v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Most existing agentic reinforcement learning methods assume single-session trajectories, where interaction proceeds within one continuously accumulated context. | **[Harness-Bench: Evaluating Agent Harnesses across Models and Realistic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27922v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are increasingly deployed as executable systems that use tools, modify workspaces, and produce concrete artifacts. Across 5,194 execution trajectories, we observe substantial variation in completion, process quality, efficiency, and failure behavior across model-harness pairings. These results suggest that agent capability should be reported at the model-harness configuration level rather than attributed to the base model alone. | **[PROBE: Learning to Audit Policy Compliance in Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22124v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern agentic systems extend language models with external tools, enabling them to complete complex tasks through multi-turn interactions. We propose PROBE (Policy Rule Observation via Behavioral Exploration), an algorithm for training LLM-based auditors that interact with deployed agents as realistic users and probe them for policy violations through multi-turn, tool-mediated dialogue. We formalize agent auditing as a two-player Markov game and propose a composite reward that balances violation discovery, behavioral plausibility, conversation progress, and policy diversity. To study training objectives and reward design, we compare GRPO, DPO, and GFlowNet-based optimization in a controlled setting. Across extensive experiments, auditors trained with PROBE uncover substantially broader and higher-yield policy violations than same-architecture zero-shot adversaries, even when the auditor is significantly smaller than the target agent. Our results show that multi-turn behavioral auditing is a distinct and necessary axis of agent evaluation, complementing capability and safety benchmarks. | **[Self-Programmed Execution for Language-Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06898v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>At the heart of a language model agent is an orchestrator program which is responsible for the state transition between consecutive turns. This paper introduces self-programmed execution (SPE), in which the model completion is itself the orchestrator program, and the harness evaluates this program but does not impose any fixed orchestration policy. Formally, an SPE state of an agentic machine is one from which a model completion can load any successor state up to an embedding, and an instance of an SPE state is one which executes the model completion as a program. In practice, the program requires a language with unusual features, as its source code doubles as the content of the model's context window. I therefore introduce Spell, a Lisp dialect in which programs can edit and re-evaluate themselves, and effectful expressions like model invocations are structured such that re-evaluating an edited program does not replay its side effects. Experiments with existing models, not trained for SPE or Spell, show that frontier models can learn Spell in-context and accomplish challenging agentic tasks. These results demonstrate how a LM can act as an agent without any fixed orchestration policy, and they raise the question of what self-orchestration strategies might be learned by a model trained for self-programmed execution. Code is available at https://github.com/lukejoconnor/spell.</p></details> | <details><summary>Camer...</summary><p>Camera-ready version; accepted at NeurIPS 2026. 77 pages, including appendices and checklist</p></details> |
| **[FAOPs: Normal Forms and an Algebra for AI Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22061v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Organizations increasingly encode operational procedures as multi-step agent skills, here called Agent Operating Procedures (AOPs), that must be traceable, auditable, reproducible, access-controlled, and bounded in time and cost, requirements that regulated sectors make mandatory. Yet AOPs are typically kept as coarse-grained files, in which steps bundle actions, copied procedures drift apart, any step may reach any tool, and refactoring goes unchecked. We present Functional Agent Operating Procedures (FAOP), a formal theory that brings database-grade discipline to AI agent workflows. FAOP recasts each workflow as a typed function over scoped state and subjects an entire library to four progressive normal forms: Decomposed (FNF-1) splits a workflow into atomic steps, Typed (FNF-2) makes its data flow explicit and type-checked, Factored (FNF-3) gives each shared procedure a single canonical definition so a change propagates instead of drifting, and Scoped (FNF-4) confines every step to least-privilege tool access. Once normalized, every atomic step is a separate entity with a declared contract and can be type-checked, scope-confined, bounded, tested, traced, and audited on its own. A FAOP algebra of provably sound equalities turns refactoring and parallelization into compiler rewrites; orthogonal extensions add verified termination (+V), optimization (+O), and time and cost bounds (+B); and an architectural-level classifier (0-3) determines whether a workflow needs AI at all. Together, these make integrity, isolation, typing, termination, cost, and optimization compile-time concerns across the whole library, not runtime hopes. We implement the theory in SkillBase, an AOP management system (what PostgreSQL is to data, SkillBase is to AOPs), together with a separate executor that enforces these guarantees at run time. A worked financial workflow illustrates the approach end to end.</p></details> | <details><summary>28 pa...</summary><p>28 pages, 2 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[SoK: Trading Agents or Market Crashers? Trading changes the threat: a compromised agent's orders move prices that other participants act on, and an attacker who profits from a crash can trigger the shock that a non-robust agent mishandles. We present FARSIGHT (Financial Agent Robustness and Security Investigation and Global Holistic Taxonomy), a systematization that rates trading agents on robustness to market stress and on security against source manipulation, agent compromise, and agents acting as attackers. It also checks paper-level ratings against released code. Across 15 academic schemes, none earns full credit on any robustness metric, no paper describes a way to halt trading without waiting for LLM inference, and none combines source provenance with cross-source corroboration. In the code of the 7 open-source schemes, no release enforces a stop-loss or position limit outside the LLM, 4 lack a component that their paper or documentation describes, and 2 run model-written code on the host. The same gaps appear beyond finance. Restating FARSIGHT as a domain-independent threat model, we map them onto agents in healthcare, law, software, and crypto. We then trace how compromised agents spread harm, as worms and as cascades that need no further compromise. At published injection rates per hop, a channel read by ten agents lets one compromised agent start an outbreak. We derive 14 open problems and urge deployers to corroborate sources, keep interrupts that bypass the model, and enforce loss limits outside it.</p></details> | <details><summary>51 pa...</summary><p>51 pages, 15 figures, 19 tables, 206 references. | **[WEAVE: Weaving Real-World Code Evolution into Dependency-Structured Tasks for Benchmarking and Training Code Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22039v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Real-world software engineering rarely consists of isolated changes. Yet most executable repository-level benchmarks retain a single issue or PR as the task unit, while existing multi-PR settings rely on coarse aggregation and structure-agnostic evaluation. This mismatch obscures dependencies among changes and limits progress measurement and learning signals for long-horizon code agents. We introduce WEAVE, a framework that transforms verified PR histories into executable, dependency-structured tasks. We further introduce Graph Credit, a deterministic, test-grounded metric that measures dependency-consistent partial progress and supports dense, verifiable rewards for reinforcement learning. Experiments on task construction, agent evaluation, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning show that WEAVE yields coherent executable tasks, enables more faithful progress measurement than flat scoring, and provides effective supervision and verifiable rewards for training code agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, including appendix</p></details> |

The reproduced inputs and results serve as ground truth for grading agents with fixed rules, without an LLM judge. | **[Orchestrating LLM Agents for Scientific Research: A Pilot Study of Multiple Choice Question (MCQ) Generation and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.18891v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Advances in large language models (LLMs) are rapidly transforming scientific work, yet empirical evidence on how these systems reshape research activities remains limited. We report a mixed-methods pilot evaluation of an AI-orchestrated research workflow in which a human researcher coordinated multiple LLM-based agents to perform data extraction, corpus construction, artifact generation, and artifact evaluation. Using the generation and assessment of multiple-choice questions (MCQs) as a testbed, we collected 1,071 SAT Math MCQs and employed LLM agents to extract questions from PDFs, retrieve and convert open textbooks into structured representations, align each MCQ with relevant textbook content, generate new MCQs under specified difficulty and cognitive levels, and evaluate both original and generated MCQs using a 24-criterion quality framework. Across all evaluations, average MCQ quality was high. However, criterion-level analysis and equivalence testing show that generated MCQs are not fully comparable to expert-vetted baseline questions. Strict similarity (24/24 criteria equivalent) was never achieved. Persistent gaps concentrated in skill\ depth, cognitive engagement, difficulty calibration, and metadata alignment, while surface-level qualities, such as {grammar fluency}, {clarity options}, {no duplicates}, were consistently strong. Beyond MCQ outcomes, the study documents a labor shift. The researcher's work moved from ``authoring items'' toward {specification, orchestration, verification}, and {governance}. Formalizing constraints, designing rubrics, building validation loops, recovering from tool failures, and auditing provenance constituted the primary activities. We discuss implications for the future of scientific work, including emerging ``AI research operations'' skills required for AI-empowered research pipelines.</p></details> |  |
| **[ORACLE-SWE: Quantifying the Contribution of Critical Information Signals on SWE Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.07789v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in Language Model (LM) agents have increasingly focused on repository-level software engineering (SWE) tasks, leading to various agentic workflows, training methods and failure analysis. Interestingly, rather than directly generating or evaluating edit patches, these approaches first examine and improve intermediate information signals collected during agent exploration, including Reproduction Test, Regression Test, Edit Location, Execution Context, and API Usage. Although different studies examine different subsets of these signals, no work has systematically compared their relative effectiveness in improving final task success or measured the remaining space for improvement in each signal. To address this gap, this work quantifies the contributions of both oracle and agent-extracted signals to final task success and measures the gap between current agent-found signals and their oracle counterparts. We hope our findings could suggest future research emphasis in SWE agents. We release our source code and oracle-signal dataset as a reusable methodology to benefit future signal analysis of SWE agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Under...</summary><p>Under peer review; 31 pages, 9 figures, 11 tables</p></details> |
| **[Predict Before You Modify: Learning Improvement Dynamics for Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13991v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Self-evolving agents improve through repeated cycles of modification and evaluation. However, existing methods receive feedback on modifications only after costly evaluations, and aggregate scores may obscure distinctions between failures that require different repairs. We introduce predictive self-evolution through an editable Improvement Dynamics Model (IDM). Before evaluation, the IDM predicts task-level outcomes of a proposed modification and guides an optional revision. After evaluation, past modifications and their observed outcomes are used to improve the IDM, allowing predictive knowledge to accumulate across generations. The system can thus improve how it solves tasks, proposes modifications, and predicts their effects. Building on HyperAgents, we evaluate this approach across multilingual coding, paper review, robotics reward design, and Olympiad-level mathematical grading. Our approach outperforms HyperAgents across all four domains, with up to 46% higher average performance over generations. Gains also persist under matched token budgets that include both agent evolution and task evaluation, and extend to held-out tasks. Forecast accuracy improves as the IDM evolves, and the learned predictive knowledge transfers to improve evolution in a new domain.</p></details> |  |
| **[OntoAct: Hypothesis-Based Neuro-Symbolic Agents through Ontological Abstraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21983v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Learning from experience requires recovering reusable knowledge and determining when it applies to a new situation. We introduce OntoAct, a hypothesis-based neuro-symbolic framework built on ontological abstraction. OntoAct turns individual experiences into axioms over entity types, relations, and conditions, making their underlying patterns reusable across situations. A large language model (LLM) uses this world knowledge to propose hypotheses and actions, while a symbolic verifier assesses their supporting conditions against current observations. Verification identifies missing evidence, and new observations and intervention outcomes update hypothesis assessments and guide subsequent actions. We also introduce Tacit-FLE, a benchmark built on the Factorio Learning Environment (FLE) for diagnosing and repairing hidden faults in existing factories using inherited operational records. Its synthetic maintenance corpus distributes evidence across records, and an executed repair may leave the fault unresolved, requiring agents to reassess their diagnoses through interaction. Across Tacit-FLE, Mars, and ALFWorld, OntoAct achieves leading task performance with efficient interaction and inference.</p></details> | <details><summary>49 pa...</summary><p>49 pages, 13 figures, 33 tables. Preprint, under review</p></details> |
| **[Inflak: A Unified Orchestration Architecture for Human-Agent Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21972v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-powered agents increasingly support open-ended activities, yet orchestrating when and how agents interact with humans remains challenging. We implement Inflak as agent-native, skill-based plugins within a general-purpose agent framework and evaluate it through a gallery of various application scenarios and interaction paradigms, technical performance testing, and a comparative implementation case study. The evaluations show that Inflak can serve as a general and extensible foundation for orchestrating diverse human-agent interaction flows.</p></details> |  |
| **[Agentic-Priors: Using Large Language Models to Address the Cold Start Problem in Preference Elicitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21938v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An agent that learns a user's preferences from their choices faces a cold start problem: before any choices are observed, it must rely solely on a prior. Informative priors are usually fitted to historical choice data, which new tasks and deployments lack. To address this, we present Agentic-Priors, which lets agentic systems use the implicit knowledge of large language models (LLMs) instead. Specifically, an LLM states a new user's trade-offs in interpretable terms, such as willingness to pay or value of time, which we map to a prior over multinomial logit coefficients. This prior is then refined by a Bayesian learner as choices are observed. Across seven choice datasets, Agentic-Priors improves cold start accuracy over an uninformed prior by 5-40 percentage points. | **[CodeHealth Predicts the Token Cost of Agentic Code Review: A Pre-Registered Study of 465 Agent-Authored Pull Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21914v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Context: Organizations increasingly delegate code review to multi-agent large language model (LLM) pipelines billed per token. Method: In a pre-registered observational study, we sampled 465 agent-authored PRs from 246 repositories in AIDev, jointly stratified by CodeHealth and footprint under repository caps. A frozen five-agent review-and-fix pipeline processed every PR with three different LLMs. Per LLM, we fitted a mixed model of log tokens on CodeHealth and log PR footprint, with a repository random intercept. Results: For all three LLMs the CodeHealth coefficient was negative, with 95% CI excluding zero; each CodeHealth point was associated with 3.5% to 6.1% lower token cost. CodeHealth was unrelated to PR footprint, so PR size does not explain the association. Two of three registered smells predicted fix cost; batch fixing left median CodeHealth unchanged. Exploratory analyses located the association in the review stage, where agents iterated more on unhealthy code without writing more comments. Adjusting for the size of the scored files absorbed the association, which we interpret as mediation by file size. Conclusions: A quality metric calibrated against human maintainability also predicts the inference cost of multi-agent review and fix. The effect is modest per point and not carried by PR size, so the refactoring case extends to agent-reviewed code when it changes structure and file size, not only the score. Data Availability: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22127920</p></details> | <details><summary>32 pa...</summary><p>32 pages, 6 figures, 16 tables</p></details> |
| **[CoWorld Reasoner: Agentic Multimodal Reasoning with the Evolving Video World Model-Driven Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21910v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As Vision-Language Models (VLMs) advance, they are increasingly expected to perform spatiotemporal reasoning over motion, actions, and view changes beyond what the input images show. Its Video World Model-Driven Harness exposes multiple visual operations through tools and uses skills to guide the VLM in deciding whether to imagine, selecting an operation, constructing a generation request, and interpreting the resulting visual evidence. Across seven benchmarks spanning spatial intelligence, temporal and causal reasoning, and perceptual reasoning, CoWorld Reasoner outperforms direct reasoning with the same VLM. Across four open-weight models, key-entity hit rates reached 75.71-97.62% overall and 96.67-100.00% on the historical-residence subset. Adding historical labels to otherwise identical, source-ordered facts increased overall hit rates by 17.14-55.95 percentage points. The evidence was compact: 94-118 tokens per answer, or 48.75-56.73% less than session-aware retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). | **[MetaShare: Failure-Aware Collective Self-Improvement for Open-Ended Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21887v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Humans often learn fastest not by repeating success, but by remembering which failures not to repeat. We introduce MetaShare, a failure-aware framework that records successful and unsuccessful agent modifications together with signed performance deltas. Before each generation, MetaShare retrieves diverse high-performing strategies alongside high-confidence failures from other lineages, letting agents reuse effective mechanisms while avoiding known dead ends. A task-blind auditor periodically injects absent or underrepresented mechanism categories, while embedding-based de-ranking limits redundancy during retrieval. Multiple meta-agents propose modifications in parallel at heterogeneous temperatures behind a deterministic selector that gates applicability, scope, compilability, and size. Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 Meta-Agents Workshop (Managing Agents that Manage Agents)</p></details> |

To test whether the artefacts carry the design, we pose a software Ship of Theseus: a coding agent rebuilds 29 Commit0 libraries from the intent alone or with the recovered requirements and architecture, given only an interface contract and part of the tests. The artefacts do not significantly change the held-out pass rate (33.7% vs. 35.8%), but bring the rebuilt module structure significantly closer to the original (module F1 from 0.54 to 0.63), with either generator and even with concise artefacts. | **[TeraAgent: A Distributed Agent-Based Simulation Engine for Simulating Half a Trillion Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24063v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent-based simulation is a method for studying highly complex systems in disciplines as diverse as biology, medicine, sociology, economics, finance, and more. In this method, an agent represents a small part of the system, such as a human cell, and, through interactions among many agents, the system's behavior emerges, like the growth of cancer in the human body. Real-world complex systems, such as a human organ, comprise billions of agents. However, the state-of-the-art agent-based simulation platform, BioDynaMo, does not support the distributed processing of a simulation across servers. This limitation restricts the scalability of agent counts to the computational resources available within a single server, hindering users from conducting larger and more meaningful simulations. In this paper, we introduce TeraAgent, a new distributed agent-based simulation engine. To overcome BioDynaMo's scalability limitation, TeraAgent enables efficient exchange of agent information across servers by implementing two solutions: 1) a tailored serialization mechanism that allows agents to be accessed and mutated directly at the receive buffer, and 2) delta encoding that significantly reduces data transfer across servers by leveraging the iterative nature of agent-based simulations. Built on our solutions, TeraAgent 1) enables extreme-scale simulations with half a trillion agents (292x that of BioDynaMo) on 84,096 CPU cores, 2) reduces time-to-result leveraging parallelism across compute nodes, 3) improves interoperability with third-party tools, and 4) provides users with more hardware flexibility. We find that very small models frequently fail to retrieve or select correct skills, substantially limiting their usefulness in the evaluated skill-augmented settings, while larger 30B--80B models gain substantial benefits from skill augmentation. Furthermore, we provide a deployment-oriented GPU profile, reporting VRAM time occupancy (GB$\cdot$min) across models under our evaluation settings---a deployment-oriented metric that may help characterize GPU-memory costs, where small and medium models are typically preferred for VRAM control. These results characterize how the benefits of skill augmentation vary with model scale, task complexity, and GPU budget, and provide deployment-oriented observations.</p></details> | 12 pages |
| **[MedPAO-VL: Interpretable and Protocol-Compliant Agent for Structured Radiology Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21794v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite the remarkable generalization capabilities of Large Language Models, generating clinically accurate and structured radiology reports from medical images remains a significant challenge. We present MedPAO-VL, a multimodal agent that shifts the paradigm from monolithic generation to explainable, iterative, protocol-compliant structured report generation. Unlike deterministic models, our plan-act-observe loop functions as a goal-oriented agent: it first perceives the Chest X-Ray through an explainable vision module designed to capture inherent diagnostic uncertainty, then iteratively refines the generated report by observing semantic discrepancies and self-correcting its generation for better alignment with visually detected clinical findings. Evaluation of our framework demonstrates that this agentic reasoning improves clinical correctness and completeness, achieving an expert-rated accuracy score of 3.5/5 and a structural score of 4.3/5. By prioritizing pathology specific explainability and iterative refinement over opaque end-to-end mapping, MedPAO-VL offers a transparent and clinically viable alternative for automated structured radiology report generation.</p></details> | <details><summary>The p...</summary><p>The paper has been accepted at ECCV BISCUIT Workshop, 2026</p></details> |
| **[RefactorPlatform: An Open-Source Harness for Controlled Evaluation of Repository-Scale Refactoring Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04898v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Repository-scale refactoring requires coding agents to propagate a single change across many interdependent files without altering program behavior, yet to our knowledge no existing harness isolates the design choices that determine agent success on this task. | **[RSIFlow: Adaptive Self-Improvement Workflows with an Evolving Meta-Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21791v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents have demonstrated strong capabilities in complex tasks, including code generation, tool use, and multi-hop question answering. The meta-agent then distills workflow outcomes into component-specific skills and cross-component principles, evolving its own harness to improve subsequent workflow orchestration. | **[Don't Regenerate, Debug: A Domain-Specific Agent for Repairing Near-Miss Accelerator Kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02712v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Kernel generation for hardware accelerators such as GPUs and NPUs has become a proving ground for large language models (LLMs), and state-of-the-art systems raise correctness through pipelines that couple LLMs with agentic reinforcement learning and evolutionary search. We argue for a paradigm shift: rather than regenerate, debug. Debugging is far more constrained than generating from scratch: the search space is small and feedback is dense. We present a domain-specific debug agent that addresses three core challenges in autonomous repair: mitigating knowledge scarcity through retrieved patterns and diagnostic instrumentation, ensuring integrity through anti-cheat detection and full-coverage evaluation, and controlling cost via convergence guards and bounded iteration. Debugging serves two complementary roles: it extends the capability frontier by recovering operators that repeated regeneration fails to produce, and it lowers cost per deliverable operator. Debug Pass@1 achieves 66.7% versus Regenerate Avg Pass@1's 25.9% and Regenerate Pass@3's 40.7%, while consuming 92.8% fewer tokens per success than three-trial regeneration. | **[From Research Goals to Scientific Closure: Evaluating Agents Through Evidence-Supported Physics Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21767v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Can AI agents organize scientific investigations that lead to well-supported conclusions? | **[How language model agents reshape price competition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21711v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Repeated Bertrand competition is a stringent benchmark for algorithmic pricing: in homogeneous-goods markets, standard learning procedures typically select the stage-game Nash outcome with prices near marginal cost. Agents that post one price every period from raw market histories that include rival prices can sustain supracompetitive regimes in self-play. Agents that periodically emit probability distributions from summarized histories of their own profits instead move toward the competitive region in self-play. Markets mixing these interfaces can generate dynamic regimes in which LLMs establish a high-price anchor and regularly undercut it to capture demand. | **[The Role of Agentic Instruction Categories for Code Generation: A Study of Five Python Libraries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21421v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic coding systems increasingly rely on repository-level agent files, such as AGENTS.md, to communicate project knowledge and developer intent. However, it remains unclear which categories of agent instructions enable efficient agent-file generation and accurate code generation. We evaluate five instruction categories: Repository Overview, Function Description, Implementation Detail, Testing, and Reconstruction Steps, across all 31 non-empty combinations using two Large Language Models (LLMs), Gemma 4 and GPT-5.4, and five human-written Python libraries. Our study addresses two research questions, comprising five sub-questions, on agent file generation and code generation. Results show that the number of instructions alone does not determine token usage for agent-file generation, with substantial variation across models and libraries. Although most AI-generated libraries compiled successfully regardless of the instruction combination, functional correctness remained limited. No Gemma 4 variant passed all tests, whereas 12.99% of GPT-5.4 variants did. Function Description achieved Gemma 4's highest partial-pass rate, while Reconstruction Steps achieved GPT-5.4's highest full-pass rate. For structural similarity to human-written code, Reconstruction Steps was most frequently associated with libraries closest to the originals in Lines of File and total Cyclomatic Complexity, while Testing and Reconstruction Steps were jointly most frequent for File Count and Function Count.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to APSEC 2026 Technical Track</p></details> |
| **[MasDrift: Benchmarking Authorization Preservation Across Multi-Agent Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07556v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent systems (MAS) decompose long-horizon tasks across supervisors and subagents, but delegated goals do not necessarily carry their original authorization boundaries. Existing safety benchmarks mainly study adversarial compromise, while work on constraint drift lacks controlled architecture-level evaluation.
