MasDrift compares single-agent, centralized, and decentralized coordination while varying hierarchy depth and peer width, measuring task completion and authorization preservation. Across generic multi-agent conditions, centralized hierarchies achieve 93.9--98.6% task completion versus 85.7--87.0% for peer networks, while unauthorized actions occur in 2.7--19.8% of tasks versus 0.6--0.8%, a gap that widens with hierarchy depth. We further compare two defenses that differ in where authorization evidence resides. One re-anchors every pending call to the original user request. The other carries an attenuated policy along the delegation chain. Re-anchoring reduces unauthorized actions in every model configuration we evaluate, at a cost of 1.6 points of pooled completion. Chain propagation blocks required work instead, forfeiting up to 36.3 points. A heterogeneous case study confirms that the failure follows from coordination rather than model strength. MasDrift exposes a centralization tradeoff and makes authorization preservation a measurable property of MAS design.</p></details> | preprint |
| **[Jev-LiteMAS: Separating Decisions from Generation in Multi-Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21411v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent LLM systems rely on generative models for expert execution, workflow planning, and artifact review. Yet many coordination operations require only bounded decisions, such as selecting the next expert or judging artifact quality. Profiling three representative MAS frameworks, we find that routing and review account for 31.7-43.5% of generative-LLM tokens. However, replacing these operations with low-cost decision models introduces two interface gaps: generative planners produce execution plans and handoff instructions (planning gap), while reviewers provide actionable feedback for repairing flawed artifacts (review gap), which bounded decisions alone cannot provide. We introduce Jev-LiteMAS, a multi-agent framework that bridges both gaps by combining a lightweight decision model (Jev) with generative execution and feedback. To address the planning gap, Jev selects experts dynamically from the task and prior expert outputs, using accumulated context to ground subsequent execution without a pre-generated trajectory. To address the review gap, Jev screens intermediate artifacts against task criteria: passing artifacts proceed directly, whereas flagged ones undergo LLM re-review for acceptance or corrective feedback. Across four benchmarks and two backbones, Jev-LiteMAS reduces generative-LLM tokens by 12.7-54.6% over LAS, maintaining accuracy within 1.34 percentage points and lowering latency in seven of eight settings. Grounding routing in substantive expert outputs prevents up to 18.0 points in accuracy loss.</p></details> |  |
| **[MemCut: Every Secret Leaves a Trail in the Long-Term Memory of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21405v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-term memory has become a crucial component of large language model (LLM) agents, with most research focusing on retrieving relevant information accurately and efficiently. However, a dependable memory system must also forget sensitive user data upon request. To evaluate this capability, we introduce MemCut, a systematic benchmark for user-requested forgetting. Its 456 hand-designed secrets in long-horizon conversations have fully annotated evidence trails to test recovery after deletion, including from messages that never state the secrets directly. We propose two recovery metrics. Native recovery measures reader success through the system's retrieval, whereas residual recovery measures the same reader's success when directly given surviving objects traced to annotated disclosures or evidence. We establish three reference deletion baselines to test how far existing retrievers and write models support forgetting across five memory designs and four LLMs. Disclosure removal reduces native recovery far more than residual recovery. LLM-guided deletion of ten additional turns from twenty retrieval candidates leaves 39-76% residual recovery. Subset sweeps (144 facts, three systems, two models) show that widening the pool at a fixed budget reduces residual recovery more than deleting all original candidates. These sweeps identify candidate coverage as a bottleneck in the reference baselines.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 4 figures, 13 tables, including appendices</p></details> |
| **[ParanoiaEval: Benchmarking Unnecessary Defensive Work in Agentic Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08662v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As coding agents increasingly undertake real-world work autonomously, judging whether their risk treatments are warranted has become important. Existing work evaluates related agent behaviors from separate perspectives, but lacks a systematic framework for unifying these behaviors. To bridge this gap, we introduce ParanoiaEval, the first benchmark for unified evaluation of risk-treatment capabilities in coding agents. Grounded in the well-established Avoidance-Transfer-Mitigation-Acceptance framework in software engineering risk management, ParanoiaEval operationalizes its 4 fundamental treatments for coding-agent settings and contains 200 evidence-controlled repository-level task pairs, each differing only in treatment-defining evidence. We further introduce dedicated metrics for risk-treatment violations and evidence responsiveness, using a human-calibrated agentic judge for reliable evaluation. Large-scale experiments on 8 representative models and a post-hoc human study reveal that (I) unnecessary risk treatment occurs in 11.2%-58.7% of runs despite explicit evidence, with substantial variation across agent configurations; (II) stronger task capability does not ensure more appropriate risk treatment, while treatment violations substantially harm developers' experience, establishing risk treatment as an independent capability dimension; and (III) agents exhibit systematic patterns consistent with established risk-management findings, suggesting that knowledge from human practice can guide the diagnosis and improvement of this capability.</p></details> |  |
| **[Matoi: Selective Revalidation of Consequence Evidence for Agent Authorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21394v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Matoi separates reusable consequence evidence from current execution authority and durable occurrence history. Its finite query-aware contract covers branches, ordered collections and negative lookups. A consumer reuses only its own checked evidence whose observations remain valid; signed requests, live grants, quota and uncertain outcomes remain separate obligations. Conditional correctness arguments distinguish locally serialized claims from nontransactional remote effects. Two studies strengthen cost attribution and domain evaluation. A 1,440-cell matched-wire study repeats 144 authored inputs. With compact input bytes identical, reuse reduces complete lifecycle cost by 3.49 percent in JS and 1.79 percent in Python. Combined representation/reuse reductions against full proof are 10.87 and 16.53 percent; five of twelve family intervals for the matched comparison cross one in each language. A prospectively frozen AgentDojo Banking mapping covers 5/16 user plans and 7/9 attack plans. Both governed strategies preserve five exact benign transfers and refuse 35 reused attack-plan/policy crosses. Pre-satisfied utility predicates are reported separately from completed work. These are original scripted synthetic tools, not autonomous agents or a real bank. Earlier studies retain cheaper protected-cache alternatives, policy-compatible phishing, provider duplication and post-claim revocation. Separate JS/Python consumers establish finite-profile interoperability within the same project; durable recovery rechecks evidence and retains uncertain reservations. The results support a bounded, auditable reuse and execution contract, not a new general incremental algorithm, arbitrary-tool closure, atomic third-party commitment or production superiority.</p></details> | <details><summary>55 pa...</summary><p>55 pages, 4 figures. Includes an 18-page technical manuscript and a 33-page detailed supplement as ancillary files. Research preprint; not peer reviewed</p></details> |
| **[PriorityFEC: Deadline-Aware Streaming FEC for KV-Cache Handoff in Edge Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21390v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent large language model (LLM) systems are increasingly deployed at the edge to support complex tasks that require collaboration and information exchange among multiple agents. However, when task contexts are passed across agents, shared context that has already been processed by upstream agents often needs to be prefilled again, resulting in substantial redundant computation. Reusing an already prefilled key--value (KV) cache can avoid this recomputation, but requires the reusable KV state to be transferred across the network before the receiving agent can begin generation. Under strict time-to-first-token (TTFT) deadlines and unreliable edge links, delivering the complete KV cache may be infeasible, making it necessary to prioritize and protect the most valuable KV information within a limited transmission budget. This paper proposes PriorityFEC, a utility-aware KV-cache handoff scheme that jointly exploits model-side KV importance and transport-side reliability. PriorityFEC first constructs a utility-aware source stream by placing high-utility KV blocks at earlier transmission positions. It then protects the ordered stream using a feedback-adaptive Streaming Forward Erasure Correction (FEC) mechanism, which schedules repair packets according to receiver feedback before the TTFT deadline. We implement PriorityFEC on a distributed edge LLM serving prototype and evaluate it with Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct across multiple datasets. Results show that PriorityFEC achieves superior task quality over the compared transport schemes, with its advantages becoming particularly pronounced under stringent deadline constraints. These results demonstrate the importance of jointly optimizing KV-cache utility and deadline-aware transport reliability when reusable LLM state must be handed off across unreliable edge networks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Agentic Dependency Transformations: Lessons from an Exploratory Case Study of Flask](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21372v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern software relies heavily on third-party libraries, requiring maintainers to balance the benefits of reuse against dependency-related maintenance and risk. We define dependency transformation as an emerging approach in which an AI coding agent alters how a repository handles external dependencies. Local reimplementation is one transformation strategy that recreates required functionality as local code; dependency transformation is broader and may include other strategies. In this emerging-results paper, we report an exploratory case study of Flask to explore dependency transformation strategies. We design experiments that show how available context shapes an agent's dependency discovery and iterative transformation of dependencies. The case yields five preliminary lessons. These lessons and the five-part research agenda position dependency transformation as an emerging research direction and motivate explicit transformation contracts, provenance constraints, preservation requirements, and independent validation criteria.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted ERA paper to APSEC2026</p></details> |
| **[Constructing and Evaluating Clinical Reasoning Trajectories for Medical Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05090v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluation of medical artificial intelligence agents remains predominantly answer-centric, assessing only the correctness of final outputs while overlooking the quality of intermediate reasoning. In clinical settings, however, a correct answer reached through fabricated evidence or incoherent logic is as dangerous as an incorrect one. We propose MedTraj, a framework that treats reasoning trajectories as critical objects for construction, evaluation, and optimization. The pipeline generates structured multi-step clinical reasoning chains from medical diagnosis sources. Each trajectory is then parsed into clinical observations, evidence, numbered reasoning steps, and a final conclusion, and scored across five quality dimensions: coherence, evidence support, hallucination, completeness, and traceability. Controlled error injection introduces targeted faults into otherwise correct trajectories to establish causal links between specific reasoning failures and measurable quality degradation. Building on this, step-level filtering based on marginal contribution identifies which individual reasoning steps drive or undermine trajectory quality. Finally, quality-weighted context learning feeds trajectory evaluations back into the model at inference time, allowing it to learn from both strong and weak reasoning demonstrations. Experiments across CareQA, PubMedQA, and CECMed demonstrate that trajectory context consistently improves reasoning coherence, with gains of +0.029 to +0.041 over a zero-shot baseline. On the CECMed severity assessment task, quality-weighted context nearly doubles the correctness over the zero-shot baseline while cutting the hallucination ratio by 87%. Marginal-contribution analysis further shows that a small minority (15.3%) of reasoning steps carry most of the quality signal, and that extending chains beyond four steps yields diminishing returns.</p></details> |  |
| **[OpCredit: Operation-Aware Policy Learning for Agentic Knowledge Graph Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21345v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic knowledge graph question answering(KGQA) relies on sequences of executable operations, but trajectory-level rewards obscure their individual contributions. Successful trajectories may contain ineffective operations, while failed trajectories may contain useful ones. To assess individual operations, we propose OpCredit, an operationaware policy-learning framework that combines downstream outcome credit with immediate transition credit: Branch Outcome Credit (BOC) reuses ordinary rollouts to compare terminal task rewards observed after different operations under matched navigation contexts; Frontier Transition Credit (FTC) measures operation-induced changes in answer-conditioned frontier potential. The two complementary signals jointly supervise the corresponding decision spans during training, while inference uses only the learned policy and standard KG interface. Experiments on ComplexWebQuestions, WebQSP, and GrailQA across three backbones demonstrate consistent improvements in KGQA performance.</p></details> |  |
| **[DocPilot: Interactive Agent Orchestration and Training for Long Visual Document Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21327v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal long-document question answering requires models to seek sparse evidence across pages and modalities, understand heterogeneous document content, and reason over the collected evidence. Even advanced vision-language models (VLMs) can overlook sparse evidence and struggle with cross-modal reasoning in a single pass. Existing document agents further introduce multi-step interaction, but some still rely on predefined workflows or coarse-grained actions, limiting the flexibility and granularity of document reasoning. We introduce DocPilot, an agentic document framework that supports expressive orchestration and makes complex document solving learnable by small language models (SLMs). DocPilot orchestrates evidence seeking through page-level navigation and fine-grained evidence localization, and integrates local visual inspection, code-based numerical reasoning, and cross-modal reasoning for multimodal understanding. To transfer these capabilities to small models, we propose curriculum-based agentic training that progresses from single-ability learning to adaptive orchestration through supervision from data-grounded reasoning traces and executable interaction trajectories. Across MMLongBench-Doc, DocBench, FinRAGBench-V, and MADQA, DocPilot achieves strong gains with frontier VLM backbones, while DocPilot-4B delivers competitive performance against substantially larger models, demonstrating strong effectiveness and generalization across diverse long-document reasoning settings. The code is available at https://github.com/HKUSTDial/DocPilot.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 13 figures, 9 tables</p></details> |
| **[Constrained Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning in Imagination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21319v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Predicting cost in a multi-agent world model moves most constrained policy updates into imagination: the stronger model-free baseline needs $10$ to $20$ times CA-MAMBA's interactions on the median SMAC configuration, and CA-MAMBA earns $2.8$ to $12$ times the baselines' MAMuJoCo reward at equal budget. In controlled tests on one task per benchmark, pricing the model's cost advantages at parity more than halves a cost-blind learner's cost and meets a tight continuous-control limit the learned multiplier misses, suggesting the model's cost estimates are not the bottleneck.</p></details> |  |
| **[TimeRSI: Towards Self-Improved Agentic Deployment for Time-Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19725v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time-series foundation models provide strong zero-shot forecast, but deployment for real-world applications still requires task-specific designs for model inputs and outputs. Direct inference can leave domain knowledge and recurring forecast errors unaddressed, while tailored pipelines require substantial human expertise and effort. We introduce TimeRSI, a self-improving agentic deployment system around frozen forecasting models. A coding agent translates task descriptions into executable pipeline changes. It tests each revision on historical forecasting windows and uses observed errors to guide subsequent revisions. During deployment, an adaptive selector chooses among the resulting pipelines using completed forecasts under similar conditions. Across 198 tasks on TIME and fev-bench, TimeRSI outperforms all submissions on six point and probabilistic forecasting metrics. Controlled comparisons support the value of feedback-guided revision over non-iterative pipeline search. Further experiments verify the generalization with different coding agents and forecasting models. Code is available at https://github.com/haoxin1998/TimeRSI.</p></details> |  |
| **[Bias Control and Injection for Multi-Agent Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21290v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent reasoning with large language models (LLMs) raises a central question for collective decision-making: when agents disagree, whose decision should be trusted? Resolving such disagreements is difficult because agents' decisions are susceptible to non-semantic biases such as majority pressure and response-style preferences, which can obscure the quality of the underlying reasoning. Our analysis shows how bias can be both mitigated and exploited: bias control improves initial answer selection, while bias injection elicits decision instability that serves as a reliability signal. Building on these findings, we introduce a test-time aggregation framework that requires neither validation data nor prior knowledge of relative model strength. The framework implements bias control by presenting one style-normalized reasoning trace per distinct answer, and bias injection by placing each aggregating agent's initially selected answer in the minority. Overall, our framework achieves higher average accuracy and better token efficiency than competing non-oracle aggregation methods across three mathematical reasoning benchmarks and four heterogeneous LLM combinations. It also improves final-answer accuracy when applied to multi-agent debate and Mixture-of-Agents, without modifying their interaction or synthesis processes.</p></details> |  |
